[02:35:16] So a lot of the literature, both in my research group and in the community, has focused on trying to get efficient estimators to the likelihood.
[02:35:24] it. And broadly, there are a few things that one can play with. So what I'll focus in this

[02:36:07] So there is a huge literature on trying to do these kinds of likelihood estimation.

[02:36:24] What this basically did was show that you can start
[02:36:27] with the sequence that's completely massed
[02:36:30] and use a very simple mean field kind of approximation
[02:36:33] where eventually the weight that you choose in this case
[02:36:37] was a simple one over the kind of waiting.
[02:36:40] So in this case, because you started with an all mass sequence,
[02:36:44] you would end up with every token having a uniform weight
[02:36:48] in this.
[02:36:49] case now the problem with this kind of approach was that there's a big train and
[02:36:53] inference mismatch because when you're doing inference you will not always be
[02:36:57] having sequences which are completely massed so while it works really well in
[02:37:03] terms of certain benchmarks when we tried to apply it for multimodal domains we found
[02:37:08] this to be a big challenge to use this kind of estimator soon after d1 there
[02:37:14] was a work called uni g rpo which uh basically
[02:37:19] tries to change the input from being fully massed to something which is actually having
[02:37:26] tokens which are randomly massed. And in this case, again, they use 1 over t. Now, this was trying
[02:37:33] to address a train inference mismatched, so you would see lots of different kinds of sequences,
[02:37:38] but it's you would have high variance because you're essentially choosing a random
[02:37:43] technique for masking. Now, what we ended up using in this work, which we found very, very
[02:37:49] useful was to actually go back to what I was mentioning earlier in this talk, which was this technique of complementary masking. So this is biased, but just as a recall of what it does was here you would sample two times steps. One would be sampled uniformly at random. The other would try to look at the complement of it. And so in this case, T2 would just be 1 minus T1. And based on these time steps, you would generate two sequences specific.
[02:38:19] specifically, the first sequence would be generated with random mass, but the second sequence
[02:38:26] would do the inverse of it.
[02:38:27] So whichever tokens were massed in the first sequence, become unmasked in the second one,
[02:38:32] and vice versa for the unmasked tokens.
[02:38:36] And doing this kind of sequence, complementary masking, we end up getting every token to be
[02:38:44] either masked in one of the two copies, and you get some kind of waiting.
[02:38:49] Now, here there was one defect in practice that we saw, which was that while trying to do
[02:38:56] reinforcement learning, where stability is extremely key to training these models at scale, you
[02:39:01] ended up getting very different weights for different sequences.
[02:39:05] So the 1 over T rating scheme was not something which was giving us good results, even though
[02:39:11] we were getting a very, very diverse set of sequences.
[02:39:16] So what we then proposed in this case was
[02:39:19] complementary masking, but using a uniform set of weights.
[02:39:23] And here, basically every sequence and every mass token
[02:39:26] gets an equal weight.
[02:39:28] And this ended up giving us an estimator, which was biased,
[02:39:33] but also low variance, and also had less mismatched
[02:39:36] between training and infants.
[02:39:38] So empirically, it was able to cover for a lot of the demerits
[02:39:43] that we saw in previous schemes.
[02:39:46] Yeah, and then applying this kind of reinforcement learning
[02:39:49] techniques help those drastically improve across a bunch of benchmarks in math reasoning,
[02:39:55] question answering, image editing, question object grounding. So this is a fun example of what you

[04:45:47] it turns out that these models are very unreliable
[04:45:50] and they get very inaccurate answers.
[04:45:52] And I think that the one issue that ends up happening
[04:45:55] with large language models is that natural language
[04:45:57] is a very rich source of information
[04:45:59] that contains very little information about the physical world.
[04:46:02] And in contrast, if we consider video generation,
[04:46:05] let's say we want to figure out how to tie a shoelace,
[04:46:08] a video gives us much more
[04:46:09] much richer information about how to tie the shoelaces than any type of natural language
[04:46:14] instruction does.
[04:46:18] And so that's one motivation for this talk.
[04:46:20] What motivation is this idea that by building some type of road model, you can get much better
[04:46:25] richer physical information.
[04:46:27] And I guess on the flip side, we can say that if we look at these current robotics systems,
[04:46:32] if we take these things like diffusion policy, they're able to do these really complex tasks
[04:46:37] for only 50 demonstrations, so you can do these pretty complex things, where you can do these
[04:46:40] things, where you can, for instance, spread this sauce on this pan.
[04:46:43] And one of the question might be, well, if we can build such powerful systems directly
[04:46:48] from using action data, then why do we need something like a world model?
[04:46:53] And I think the big issue is that it turns out for a lot of these existing policy systems,
[04:46:57] they actually require you to gather a lot of, a lot of demonstrations in order for a system to work.
[04:47:03] And if you look at current robotics decision-making systems, almost all of them are essentially policies,
[04:47:09] And one way to view a policy is some type
[04:47:11] of amortized decision-making system
[04:47:13] that directly takes an image and a goal,
[04:47:15] and it directly predicts actions.
[04:47:18] And policies have a couple of different advantages.
[04:47:20] The biggest one is that they're really relatively fast to run,
[04:47:23] and they're pretty simple to train.
[04:47:26] But they have a lot of issues in the sense
[04:47:28] that they really only work when you have a lot of training
[04:47:30] demonstrations showing you how to solve a task.
[04:47:33] They really require very carefully curated data,
[04:47:36] and they have to be directly grounded on the actions
[04:47:39] your particular robot.
[04:47:40] So it's pretty hard to imagine having a policy transfer
[04:47:42] across many different tasks.
[04:47:44] And in contrast, if we try to learn some type of model,
[04:47:48] essentially some type of world model, which
[04:47:49] takes in some observation and action and predicts the future,
[04:47:53] this gives you a pretty nice way to make decisions.
[04:47:55] So one nice thing is that allows you to use
[04:47:58] some type of inference time search or planning
[04:48:00] to find the actions to solve a task.
[04:48:02] It allows you to train this model on all types of data,
[04:48:04] not only success data, but also failure data.

[04:48:22] to solve different tasks.

[04:48:30] models plus reasoning in the form of planning or search really gives you a nice, effective
[04:48:34] combination that allows you to generalize a variety of different unseen things.

[07:06:38] their effects? So typically the way latent action or lead models are designed is they take in

[07:07:57] the future frame, which it shouldn't be able to.

[07:10:38] Okay, so today I'm going to talk about a very simple method for generating intermediate waypoints.
[07:10:43] for guidance when planning with latent world models.
[07:10:46] And as some initial background,
[07:10:49] there have recently been a bunch of different methods
[07:10:51] that have developed world models trained in latent space.
[07:10:54] To plan, these models use a terminal goal matching cost,
[07:10:57] which is effective but often provides a little signal
[07:10:59] for useful intermediate states during long horizon planning.
[07:11:04] So we wonder, is there a way to leverage the latent space
[07:11:06] to produce more useful intermediate waypoints for planning?
[07:11:11] Let's first consider a simple setting where we want to control a robot arm to pick and place a cube.
[07:11:17] So we're given a start and a goal frame, and we must choose our sequence of actions to move it from start to goal.
[07:11:23] So we might start by embedding our start and end frames using a frozen world model encoder.
[07:11:29] And now that we have our start and end frames as latent states, what might a simple method be for producing a plausible latent trajectory between these points that could provide effective intermediate guidance?
[07:11:41] And so a simple one might just be linearly interplating between these two states.
[07:11:48] But unfortunately, this isn't actually very good.
[07:11:51] If you notice when we decode the intermediate points found through linear interpolation back to image frames,
[07:11:57] they lose coherence in the middle of the trajectory, which is exactly where you would want additional guidance.
[07:12:03] And in practice, it doesn't really improve success rate very much to use them at all.
[07:12:08] But what if we just model the error term on top of simple linear interpret?
[07:12:11] which is essentially the method we propose.
[07:12:14] So during training, we use the trajectories from training the original world model.
[07:12:19] And for each trajectory, we encode the start and end frames,
[07:12:22] as well as intermediate frames using a frozen world model encoder.
[07:12:26] And then to predict each intermediate frame,
[07:12:29] we first compute the prediction from the linear path between start and end,
[07:12:34] and then we train a model just to predict the residual to the true intermediate latent.
[07:12:40] And so at inference time, we can generate waypoints recursively by using the last produced
[07:12:45] waypoint as a new starting point.
[07:12:47] And for planning, we incorporate this with an additional weighted cost function that encourages
[07:12:52] the model to pass near the waypoints at some point during the plan.
[07:12:58] So to evaluate, we consider a few different tasks for navigation and manipulation.
[07:13:02] For navigation, we use two rooms, where an agent must navigate from one room to a target
[07:13:06] in another room through a doorway.
[07:13:09] manipulation, we use push T, where we have to push a T-shaped block into a target pose, as well as a cube task earlier, where we have to pick and place a cube using a robot arm.
[07:13:19] We can revisit our previous experiment and ask whether our waypoints are visually coherent now, and we find that when we decode our waypoints back into images, not only are they more coherent than those from linear interpolation, but they also seem to track the Oracle trajectory, which gives us some initial signal. They might provide a more effective intermediate guidance for our model during planning.
[07:13:39] than those found through linear interpolation.
[07:13:43] Well, we can confirm this by just using them directly during planning,
[07:13:46] and we can compare to some additional baselines.
[07:13:49] For example, in addition to the linear interpolation,
[07:13:52] we might also snap each linear waypoint to the nearest training trajectory frame
[07:13:57] to test whether or not linear waypoints suffer from being off manifold for the model.
[07:14:02] So when we use our waypoints for guidance,
[07:14:04] we find that the latent flow waypoints improve performance in all tasks,
[07:14:08] but slightly less so for push-difference.
[07:14:09] and they're particularly beneficial in the cube task where they lead to a 13.8 percentage point increase in success rate.
[07:14:18] We can also just compare directly with Oracle guidance by embedding intermediate frames from the true trajectory,
[07:14:24] and we find that we can approach their performance with purely generated waypoints from our trained model.
[07:14:31] This also extends the more difficult planning scenarios, such as when we want to plan under a limited budget,
[07:14:37] and we can actually plan more efficiently just by.
[07:14:39] by using these waypoints for intermediate guidance.
[07:14:42] So we show this in particular by reducing
[07:14:45] the number of CEM optimizer steps
[07:14:47] and the number of samples available to the model
[07:14:49] and we find consistent benefits with the weakest results
[07:14:52] in push T.
[07:14:54] Finally, we can extend this to solving more difficult tasks
[07:14:57] by filtering our cube data set to create buckets
[07:15:00] which involve higher cube displacement.
[07:15:02] We find that we can provide consistent benefits
[07:15:05] over final goal planning in all scenarios,
[07:15:07] even though in both cases, performance,
[07:15:09] degrades as tasks become more difficult. Future work is looking a little bit more into better ways
[07:15:18] to place our intermediate waypoints, as currently they're just done through kind of evenly space
[07:15:23] points along the latent path. Additionally, we might want more adaptive methods as we get towards
[07:15:29] more long horizon and more difficult tasks. Finally, there might also be better ways to handle
[07:15:35] cases where we have multiple valid solution trajectories and we want to model all of them, as opposed

[07:43:01] the stuff he can predict and eliminate all the stuff

[07:44:48] about the world. It's like when you do a simulation of the fluid, you know, around an airplane,
